SLIDE S49 — A visual path for learning with AI
Page 4 / 198
10 November 2026 · Mykolas Romeris University · 12:30–14:10 · Room III-326
Who is it for?
University students and teachers. Make initial knowledge and decisions about learning visible.
What will we do?
Set a goal, map what we know, plan next steps and check AI suggestions against reliable sources. Use concept maps, reflection and self-assessment.
What will you take away?
A reusable activity template and practical tasks for autonomy, critical thinking, uncertainty and managing frustration.
[PROGRAM02] Public lecture workshop 10 November 2026 — LT/EN (2026). Brief della sessione di Marco Cremaschi.

Descrizione programmata dell’evento; non è una prova di efficacia didattica.

Page 5 / 198
11 November 2026 · EDU Forum · 15:45–17:15 · Hall 210
Who is it for?
Education researchers, teachers, school leaders, doctoral students and policymakers interested in self-directed learning, online and in blended settings.
What will we do?
Use classroom scenarios, learning-path maps and reflective prompts. Examine goals, planning, information organisation, feedback and self-assessment.
What will you take away?
Adaptable strategies and visual scaffolds for autonomy, critical thinking, emotional awareness and digital responsibility.

SLIDE S51 — Andrea needs to understand GenAI
Page 9 / 198
One student’s learning process, from assignment to assessment
Andrea is a second-year computer science student: he can program, but still confuses key concepts of generative models.
He must study a chapter and explain how a small application generates text.
We observe his use of tools and the evidence of understanding he keeps.
You are following the learning method; you do not need to learn programming.

SLIDE S52 — The student’s assignment
Page 10 / 198
Four connected products, with criteria shared before the task
1. Read
Identify concepts, relationships and uncertainties in the chapter.
2. Represent
Build a commented diagram of the model.
3. Create
Use AI if needed to obtain a text-generation application.
4. Explain
Predict a change and correct an error without delegating the answer.
A working solution is one product; an explanation provides different evidence.

SLIDE S52SDL — What is self-directed learning?
Page 11 / 198
Self-directed learning is a process in which learners take the initiative to identify learning needs, set goals, choose resources and strategies, and evaluate outcomes.
1. Identify a goal
What do I need to learn, and what do I want to achieve?
2. Choose a path
Which resources, people and strategies will help me learn?
3. Evaluate progress
What have I learned, and what should I change? Self-assessment helps here.
Andrea’s example: “I need to distinguish training from inference. I will use the chapter and a concept map, then check whether I can explain the difference.”
Teachers and peers can support the process; the learner takes responsibility for the choices.
[EDU_SELF_DIRECTED] National Library of Medicine. (n.d.). Self-Directed Learning as Topic (MeSH D000073819). Definition attributed to Knowles (1975), Self-directed learning: A guide for learners and teachers. New York: Association Press.
https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/mesh/2023167
Conceptual definition, not an efficacy trial. Knowles’s definition was verified through the NLM entry; the full original book was not consulted. The three groups and Andrea’s example are workshop illustrations.

SLIDE S52SA — What is self-assessment?
Page 12 / 198
Self-assessment means judging your own work and learning against explicit criteria, then using that judgment to improve.
1. Check the criteria
What should I be able to explain or do?
2. Look for evidence
What can I demonstrate? Where do I still struggle?
3. Choose a next step
What will I revise, practise or check again?
Andrea’s example: “I can explain what a token is, but I still confuse training and inference. I will revise that link in my map and explain it again.”
AI can offer feedback; the learner checks the evidence and decides what to improve.
[EDU_SELF_ASSESSMENT] Andrade, H. L. (2019). A critical review of research on student self-assessment. Frontiers in Education, 4, 87.
https://doi.org/10.3389/feduc.2019.00087
Definition and review of 76 empirical studies; not validation of this workshop, its three-step illustration or AI feedback. Andrea’s example is a constructed teaching scenario.

SLIDE S52EKESTIN — AI tutoring can improve learning
Page 13 / 198
An experiment in university physics
194 eligible students at Harvard · Two physics lessons · Immediate post-tests with a purpose-built tutor.
Observed benefit
The AI-tutor condition produced higher immediate test scores than an active-learning class, with less time on task.
How it was designed
Worked solutions, step-by-step guidance and personalised feedback supported students as they solved problems.
Chapter study: ask for hints, then explain training and inference without AI.
Original first page of Kestin et al. (2025), AI tutoring outperforms in-class active learning.
Kestin et al. · Scientific Reports · 2025 · p. 1.
[EDU_KESTIN] Kestin, G., Miller, K., Klales, A., Milbourne, T., & Ponti, G. (2025). AI tutoring outperforms in-class active learning: An RCT introducing a novel research-based design in an authentic educational setting. Scientific Reports, 15(1), Article 17458. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-97652-6
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-97652-6
194 eligible university physics students; tutor designed for specific lessons and immediate tests. Does not establish universal or long-term chatbot effectiveness.

SLIDE S52EDENG — What do studies find overall?
Page 14 / 198
69 studies reviewed · 62 included in meta-analysis
Mostly university students; subjects, interventions and assessment methods varied considerably.
Positive overall pattern
The review reports higher academic performance and more positive motivation-related outcomes with ChatGPT-supported activities.
Limits of the evidence
High variation, publication bias and weak assessment designs limit what the pooled results establish about lasting understanding.
Chapter study: use explanations and practice questions, then check understanding against the book.
Original first page of Deng et al. (2025), Does ChatGPT enhance student learning? A systematic review and meta-analysis of experimental studies.
Deng et al. · Computers & Education · 2025 · p. 1.
[EDU_DENG] Deng, R., Jiang, M., Yu, X., Lu, Y., & Liu, S. (2025). Does ChatGPT enhance student learning? A systematic review and meta-analysis of experimental studies. Computers & Education, 227, Article 105224. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compedu.2024.105224
https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compedu.2024.105224
Experimental and quasi-experimental studies from 2022–2024, mostly university settings. High heterogeneity (academic performance I² = 91.789%), publication bias and post-intervention assessment concerns; improved assisted output is not proof of lasting independent learning.

SLIDE S52E — Better answers can hide weaker learning
Page 15 / 198
Practice with AI and performance without AI are different outcomes
Nearly 1,000 high-school students · One Turkish school · Four mathematics sessions; short-term outcomes.
During assisted practice
GPT Base improved practice scores by 48% relative to the control group.
Later, without AI
The same group scored 17% lower than the control. The safeguarded tutor largely removed this penalty, without a clear independent-test gain.
Chapter study: attempt a problem before asking for hints; finish with an independent self-test.
First page of Bastani and colleagues’ published PNAS paper, Generative AI without guardrails can harm learning: Evidence from high school mathematics.
Bastani et al. · PNAS · 2025 · p. 1.
[EDU_BASTANI] Bastani, H., Bastani, O., Sungu, A., Ge, H., Kabakcı, Ö., & Mariman, R. (2025). Generative AI without guardrails can harm learning: Evidence from high school mathematics. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 122(26), Article e2422633122. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.2422633122
https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.2422633122
RCT in high-school mathematics at one Turkish school, with nearly 1,000 students. Separates assisted practice from subsequent unassisted tests; does not evaluate this workshop or these commercial tools.

SLIDE S53TYPES — Formal commands and informal shortcuts
Page 20 / 198
The interface recognises an action; a prompt describes a task.
Formal · interface action
An action offered by the app’s command menu. Select it to invoke the supported behaviour. Available actions depend on the app, account and enabled features.
Informal · prompt label
A label such as /flashcard or /quiz used in a prompt. The four overview tables show the 30 tested interactive outputs; individual slides also document 49 static outcomes. Specify the task, source and format: the label alone does not guarantee a widget.
79 labels tested · 30 interactive outputs catalogued · 49 static outcomes documented individually.
[CHATGPT_WEB_COMMANDS] OpenAI. (2026). Developer commands. Scope note for ChatGPT web.
https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/developer-commands
The documentation establishes the surface distinction; the seven displayed web menu actions come from the separately recorded account observation.
[CHATGPT_PROMPTING] OpenAI. (2026). Prompting: Write useful prompts for Chat, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.
https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/prompting
Guidance for writing requests, not recognition or validation of the 79 informal shortcut names.

SLIDE S53FORMALWEB — Study
Page 21 / 198
Open the guided learning action
Study: Open the guided learning action
Actual ChatGPT response
Using only the attached book, help me understand why adding text to context does not update model parameters. Ask me one short question at a time and wait for my answer. Answer in English.
Let's work through this using Section 1.2: Data, Parameters and Training of your book.
The book distinguishes between training, which changes model parameters, and inference, which uses the parameters already available.
Question 1:
If you add the sentence “In this chapter, X means Y” to a prompt, what do you think changes: the model's parameters or the information available in its context?
Explain briefly in your own words.

SLIDE S53FORMALREADALOUD — Read aloud · ChatGPT web
Page 22 / 198
Listen to the latest response
Read aloud: Listen to the latest response
Read aloud in action
Select Read aloud from the / menu for the latest response.
Actual ChatGPT Read aloud interaction: open the slash menu, choose Read aloud, see the active playback control, then return to the ready state. Silent video.
Original ChatGPT interaction · menu → playback · no audio track.
Read aloud — actual ChatGPT menu and playback controls

This silent video is edited from original UI captures, with reading holds. It does not include the spoken audio.

PROMPT
Explain the difference between training and inference in two short sentences. Answer in English.

RESPONSE
Training is the process of learning patterns from data by adjusting a model’s parameters to reduce prediction errors. Inference is the process of using those learned parameters to generate predictions or outputs for new inputs without changing them.

00:00:00.000 ChatGPT provides a short explanation of training and inference.
00:00:03.000 Open the / menu and choose Read aloud (Leggi ad alta voce).
00:00:07.000 ChatGPT loads the read-aloud playback.
00:00:09.000 The active stop control shows that read-aloud playback has started.
00:00:14.000 After playback finishes, the Read aloud control is available again.

SLIDE S53DETAILQUIZ — /quiz
Page 28 / 198
Answer questions, review feedback and move through a quiz.
New temporary chat: upload the chapter, send the shortcut, then use the generated widget.
Recorded prompt: /quiz Using only the attached chapter, create an interactive three-question quiz about tokens, context and parameters. Show feedback and let me advance one question at a time. Answer in English.
Recorded interactive output: New temporary chat → PDF → shortcut → generation → extended widget use
Earlier recording: https://chatgpt.com/c/6aec84fe-7e84-83eb-90e5-259804e72042
Real ChatGPT /quiz execution: fast prompt entry, sending, accelerated generation and normal-speed widget use.
New temporary chat → PDF → shortcut → generation → extended widget use
/quiz — fresh temporary chat, PDF, shortcut and extended widget use

FIRST MESSAGE
Attach Fondamenti_GenAI_Cremaschi.pdf.
Use the attached chapter as the only source for the next activity. Please wait for my next request.

SECOND MESSAGE
/quiz Using only the attached chapter, create an interactive three-question quiz about tokens, context and parameters. Show feedback and let me advance one question at a time. Answer in English.

SLIDE S53DETAILMINDMAP — /mindmap
Page 35 / 198
Organise a topic into branches and relationships.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /mindmap Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response · excerpt: Mind Map: Training vs. Inference

Based on Section 1.2 and Figure 1 (pages 5–6) of the attached chapter.

Language Models

How information changes model behaviour

Three distinct processes

1. Training

Examples → Prediction error → Parameter updates → Changed model

Changes learned parameters

2. Inference

Input + Fixed model → Calculations → Output distribution → Selected token

Uses existing parameters

3. Retrieval (RAG)

Question → Retrieved passages → Context + Fixed model → Generated answer

Changes available information, not weights

Key distinction: Training changes the model; inference uses it; retrieval supplies additional context…

Recorded prompt: /eli5 Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response · excerpt: Training vs. Inference — Explained Simply

Imagine a language model as a student who has already studied for an exam.

1. Training = Learning

The student studies examples and corrects mistakes. Similarly, a model compares predictions with actual tokens and updates its parameters to reduce prediction error.

2. Inference = Taking the Exam

The student answers using what they have learned. Similarly, a model generates responses using its existing parameters, without updating them.

3. Context = Having Reference Notes

Giving the student extra notes can change their answers without changing what they previously learned. Likewise, adding text or…

Concept Quiz: Training, Inference and Context

Perfect score! You got all 3 questions right.

Andrea trains a new version of a language model using examples and prediction error. What changes as a result of optimisation?

Section 1.2 explains that optimisation changes parameters to reduce aggregate prediction error.

Andrea adds a new definition to his prompt. The model uses that definition in its response. Which conclusion is justified?

Section 1.2 explicitly explains that a new definition in the conversation may influence the answer through context without updating the weights.

Andrea keeps the same model but replaces 'The capital of France is' with 'In my story, the imaginary capital of France is called'. The continuation changes. What is the best interpretation?

Section 1.7 uses this example to show that context changes the task, including whether a continuation is fictional.

SLIDE S53DETAILSUMMARY — /summary
Page 41 / 198
Condense a source into its main ideas.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /summary Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response · excerpt: Summary: Training vs. Inference

Based on §1.2, Data, Parameters and Training, of the attached chapter.

1. Training — Changes the model

The model compares predictions with actual tokens. Optimisation updates numerical parameters to reduce prediction error.

2. Inference — Uses the model

The model processes input using its existing parameters, calculates output probabilities and selects tokens. Ordinary inference does not update weights.

3. Context and retrieval — Supply information

Adding messages or retrieving documents changes the information available during inference, not the model's parameters.

Key distinction

Process

Question 2 of 3

§1.2, pp. 5–6
Does a prompt teach the model?

OBSERVED RESULT AFTER USE
Interactive Blackboard: Training vs. Inference

Explore how a language model learns during training and generates outputs during inference. Select a filter to focus on one process, then reveal the three understanding checks to test your knowledge.

Source: Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence, §1.2, pp. 5–6.

Both
Training
Inference

Training

Training examples

Actual tokens

Predictions

Compare with examples

Prediction error

Learning signal

Parameter updates

Optimisation

Changed model

Inference

Current input + context

Information available now

Model calculations

Using existing parameters

Output distribution

Next-token probabilities

Parameters remain fixed

No training update

Selected output

The key distinction
Training changes the model's parameters. Ordinary inference uses existing parameters to produce an output from the current input and context.

Three understanding checks

3 of 3 explanations revealed

Hide all

1

A model uses a new definition provided in a prompt. Have its weights necessarily changed?

§1.2, p. 5
Hide answer

Expected explanation

No. The definition changes the information available in the current context. It does not demonstrate a parameter update.

2

What is updated when the system learns from prediction errors during pretraining?

§1.2, p. 5
Hide answer

Expected explanation

The model's numerical parameters are updated through optimisation to reduce aggregate prediction error.

3

How can a different prompt change the output if the model's parameters remain fixed?

§1.2, pp. 5–6
Hide answer

Expected explanation

Ordinary inference uses the current input and context with fixed parameters. Changing the context can therefore change the output distribution without retraining.

Final takeaway: Changing context changes the information available to inference; changing parameters requires training or fine-tuning. A different answer alone is not evidence that the model has learned new weights.

Source: §1.2, Table 1 and Figure 1, pp. 5–6. The diagram follows the chapter's conceptual distinction between training and ordinary inference.

Recorded static response · excerpt: Timeline: Training vs. Inference

Based on §§1.2 and 1.7 of the attached chapter. This is a conceptual sequence, not a timeline measured in seconds.

Training — The model learns

Examples → Predictions → Error → Optimisation → Parameter updates

The model's numerical parameters change to reduce prediction error.

Inference — The model generates

Input → Fixed parameters → Output probabilities → Selected token

The model uses existing parameters. Each generated token can be appended to the context for the next prediction.

Context and retrieval — New information is supplied

Question → Retrieved passages → Context + Fixed model → Answer

Three key distinctions

SLIDE S53DETAILANALOGY — /analogy
Page 54 / 198
Explain a concept through a comparison, including its limits.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /analogy Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response · excerpt: Training vs. Inference: Three Analogies

Based on Section 1.2 of the attached chapter.

1. Training = Studying for an exam

A student learns from examples and corrects mistakes. Similarly, training reduces prediction error by updating the model's parameters.

2. Inference = Taking the exam

The student applies what they have already learned. Similarly, inference uses existing parameters to calculate an output without updating them.

3. Context = Reading an additional note

A student receives a new definition and uses it to answer a question. The information changes what is available, not what was previously learned.

Key distinction: Training…

SLIDE S53DETAILFEYNMAN — /feynman
Page 56 / 198
Explain an idea simply, identify gaps and revise the explanation.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /feynman Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response · excerpt: Feynman Technique: Training vs. Inference

Explain these three concepts as if teaching someone with no AI background.

1. Training — Learning

A model predicts tokens, compares predictions with actual examples, and updates its numerical parameters to reduce error.

2. Inference — Using what was learned

The model processes an input using its existing parameters and produces output probabilities. Ordinary inference does not update those parameters.

3. Context — Providing new information

Adding text or retrieved documents can change the model's answer without changing its parameters.

Test your understanding

Explain, in your own words:

“If…

SLIDE S53DETAILGLOSSARY — /glossary
Page 57 / 198
Collect key terms with brief definitions.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /glossary Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Glossary: Training vs. Inference

Based on Section 1.2 and the glossary of the attached chapter.

Term



Definition




Training



The process of updating model parameters to reduce prediction error.




Inference



Running a model using its existing parameters, without updating them.




Context



Information made available to the model during the current inference.

Key distinction: Training changes the model itself. Inference uses the existing model, while changes to context can influence its output without modifying its parameters.

Language Model

Three distinct processes

Training

Examples

Prediction error

Parameter updates

Changes model

Inference

Input + fixed model

Calculations

Selected output

Uses model

Retrieval

Question

Retrieved passages

Context + fixed model

Supplies information

Activity 1: Training

§1.2 · pp. 5–6

Explain what changes when a model is trained.

Recall from memory

What happens to model parameters during training, and what provides the learning signal?
Hide expected answer

Expected answer

Training compares predicted distributions with tokens in examples. The text provides the learning signal, and optimisation updates numerical parameters to reduce aggregate error.

Key distinction: Training changes the model's parameters.

Source: Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence, §1.2 · pp. 5–6

SLIDE S53DETAILFISHBONE — /fishbone
Page 65 / 198
Explore possible causes of a problem in a cause-and-effect diagram.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /fishbone Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Fishbone Diagram: Training vs. Inference

Based on §§1.2 and 1.7 of the attached chapter.

Central question: What changes when a language model learns, generates, or receives new information?

Three distinct mechanisms affecting model behavior
Three key distinctions

Training: Optimisation changes numerical parameters to reduce prediction error.

Inference: The model uses existing parameters to calculate probabilities and generate tokens.

Context / RAG: New messages or retrieved passages influence generation without necessarily changing parameters.

Takeaway: A changed answer does not prove the model has been retrained.

SLIDE S53DETAILPYRAMID — /pyramid
Page 67 / 198
Arrange ideas into levels of a hierarchy.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /pyramid Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Learning Pyramid: Training vs. Inference

Based on Section 1.2 of the attached chapter.

Conceptual learning layers, not a hierarchy of model operations.

1. Training — Changes the model

Prediction error drives parameter updates.

2. Inference — Uses the model

Existing parameters produce an output distribution and selected output.

3. Context — Supplies information

New messages or retrieved passages can affect inference without updating parameters.

Key takeaway: Training changes parameters; inference uses them; context changes the information available during inference.

SLIDE S53DETAILANATOMYDIAGRAM — /anatomydiagram
Page 69 / 198
Break a system into parts and explain their roles.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /anatomydiagram Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Anatomy Diagram: Training vs. Inference

Based on Section 1.2 and Figure 1 of the attached chapter.

Three computational paths shown in the chapter, with their main stages labeled.

Three distinctions to remember:

Training updates learned parameters to reduce prediction error.

Inference calculates outputs using fixed parameters.

RAG retrieves external passages and supplies them as context without itself changing parameters.

The critical difference is parameter updates versus using an existing model.

SLIDE S53DETAILCYCLEDIAGRAM — /cyclediagram
Page 70 / 198
Represent the stages of a repeating process.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /cyclediagram Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Cycle Diagram: Training vs. Inference

Based on Section 1.2 and Figure 1 of the attached chapter.

Inference loop

Input + fixed model → probabilities → selected token → append to context → repeat.

Parameters remain fixed.

Retrieval

Retrieve passages → add to context → generate an answer.

Does not itself update weights.

Key distinction: Training repeatedly optimizes model parameters. Inference repeatedly predicts tokens using fixed parameters. Retrieval supplies additional context without retraining the model.

OBSERVED RESULT AFTER USE
Interactive decision matrix: Training, Inference & Retrieval

Explore what changes in each process, what stays fixed, and how to identify the correct process in a practical scenario.

Source: Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence, §§1.2 and 1.7, pp. 5–6 and 13–14.

1. Select a process
Training
Inference
RAG
Training / fine-tuning

PROCESS FLOW

Examples → prediction error → parameter updates → changed model

WHAT CHANGES

The model's numerical parameters are updated through optimisation.

WHAT STAYS FIXED / IS NOT ESTABLISHED

The chapter does not specify a universal set of other components that remain fixed during training.

EXAMPLE

A system trains a new model version by optimising its parameters.
2. Comparison matrix

Dimension



Training



Inference

Final takeaway: Training changes the model's parameters; ordinary inference uses fixed parameters with the available input; RAG retrieves passages to enrich that context without itself retraining the model. Neither context changes nor retrieval guarantee a correct answer.

SLIDE S53DETAILMNEMONIC — /mnemonic
Page 76 / 198
Create a memorable cue for recalling information.
Static text response observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /mnemonic Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Mnemonic: Training vs. Inference

Remember the 3 U's: Update, Use, Unchanged.

1. Training = UPDATE

Prediction errors drive optimization, which updates the model's learned parameters.

2. Inference = USE

The model uses existing parameters and current context to produce predictions.

3. Retrieval = UNCHANGED

Retrieved passages enrich the context; model parameters remain unchanged.

Memory phrase: “Training updates, inference uses, retrieval informs.”

Quick recall: Uploading a book can change the information available to a model, but does not automatically train it.

Based on Section 1.2 and Figure 1, pages 5–6, of the attached chapter.

The machine processes a new input using its existing settings to produce an output.

Additional information becomes available without modifying the machine's internal settings.

Three concepts from Section 1.2 and Figure 1 of the attached chapter.

Training changes learned numerical values to reduce prediction error.

Input + Fixed model → Calculations → Output

Inference uses existing parameters without updating them.

Retrieval supplies external information without itself updating model parameters.

Recorded static response · excerpt: Deep Dive: Training vs. Inference

Based on §§1.2 and 1.7 of Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence.

1. Training: How the model learns

During training, the model predicts tokens and compares its predicted distribution with the tokens actually present in training examples.

Training examples

Prediction error

Optimisation

Updated model parameters

Parameters are numerical values used in the model's transformations. Optimisation changes these values to reduce aggregate prediction error.

2. Inference: How the model generates

Inference uses parameters that are already available. It does not ordinarily modify them.

Input + fixed…

SLIDE S53DETAILCOMMONWRONG — /commonwrong

Recorded static response · excerpt: Common Misconceptions: Training vs. Inference

Three common mistakes from Sections 1.2 and 1.7 of the attached chapter.

1. “Training and inference both update model parameters.”

Incorrect: Both processes modify the model.

Correction: Training updates parameters through optimization to reduce prediction error. Ordinary inference uses already available parameters without updating them.

2. “Uploading a document automatically trains the model.”

Incorrect: New information necessarily becomes part of the learned weights.

Correction: Uploaded or retrieved information may become available through context. This does not itself update model…

Recorded static response · excerpt: Real-World Applications: Training vs. Inference

Three practical scenarios from the attached chapter, especially §§1.2, 1.6 and 1.7.

1. Improving a language model — Training

Developers use examples and prediction errors to optimise numerical parameters.

What changes: The model's learned parameters.

2. Generating an answer — Inference

Andrea enters “The capital of Italy is”. The model calculates next-token probabilities using existing parameters.

What changes: The generated output, not the learned parameters.

3. Answering from documents — RAG

An application retrieves relevant passages and supplies them as context for generation.

Language model

Learned numerical parameters

updates

Parameters

Prediction error drives optimization.

uses

Fixed parameters

Calculations produce an output.

supplies

Context

External passages inform inference.

OBSERVED RESULT AFTER USE
Interactive Case Study: Andrea’s Three Experiments

Source: Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence, Chapter 1, §§1.2 and 1.7, pp. 5–6 and 13–14.

Andrea is a second-year computer science student developing a text-generation application. He wants to understand what happens when he changes a model through training, adds information to a prompt, or retrieves external passages.

Your task: Make three written decisions. For each experiment, explain what changes, what stays fixed, and what the observed result actually proves. Reveal the expected explanation after submitting your reasoning.

Case study progress

3 of 3 completed

1. Training
2. Prompt
3. RAG

Experiment 1 — Training

Andrea trains a new version of his model using examples and prediction error. During optimisation, numerical parameters are updated.

What changes in this experiment? How is it different from ordinary inference?

§1.2, pp. 5–6
Explanation revealed

Expected explanation

Training changes the model's parameters through optimisation. During ordinary inference, the model performs calculations using already available parameters. Training a new version therefore changes the model itself; changing only the input does not.

Self-check

Did you distinguish parameter updates from calculations with fixed parameters?

Previous
Next experiment

All three decisions completed

You have compared parameter updates, prompt context and retrieved context. Can you now explain all three distinctions without looking at the expected answers?

Restart case study
Final comparison

Intervention



What changes?



Model parameters




Training or fine-tuning



Learned numerical values



Updated




New prompt



Available inference context



Fixed




RAG



External passages supplied as context



Fixed

This comparison follows Figure 1 and Table 1 of the chapter (§1.2, pp. 5–6). The retrieval limitations are explained in §1.7, p. 14.

Reflection: An answer changes after Andrea adds information. What evidence would you need before claiming that the model itself has changed?

Explore three learning stations based exclusively on Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence, §§1.2 and 1.7 (pp. 5–6, 13–14). At each station, explain your reasoning in writing, then reveal the expected answer and mark the station complete.

Learning stations

3 of 3 completed
1. Training
2. Inference
3. Context
Training

§1.2 · pp. 5–6

Andrea trains a language model using examples. What changes during training, and how does prediction error contribute to that change?

Thinking prompt

Explain the relationship between examples, prediction error, optimisation and parameters.

Your explanation
Hide expected answer

Expected answer

During training, the model compares its predicted distribution with the tokens in examples. Optimisation reduces aggregate prediction error by updating numerical parameters. Training therefore changes the model itself. (§1.2, pp. 5–6)

Self-check

Did you explain both parameter updates and the role of prediction error?

I compared my explanation and completed this station.
Previous
Next station

All three stations complete.

Try explaining all three distinctions again without looking at the expected answers.

Restart activity

Final synthesis challenge: Explain in one sentence what changes during training, what stays fixed during ordinary inference, and what retrieval adds to context. This brings together Figure 1 (§1.2, p. 6) and the RAG discussion (§1.7, p. 14).

SLIDE S53DETAILSLIDES — /slides
Page 106 / 198
Outline a sequence of presentation slides.
Static PowerPoint file observed in the reviewed run.
Recorded prompt: /slides Using only the attached chapter, help university students review training versus inference. Keep the result compact; use three items where appropriate. Answer in English.
Recorded static response: Created a compact 3-slide PowerPoint for university students, based exclusively on the attached chapter.

 Download the PowerPoint

The slides cover:

Training — How prediction errors drive parameter updates.

Inference — How fixed parameters produce next-token probabilities and outputs.

Context and RAG — How new information influences answers without retraining, including a comparison of all three processes.

Key takeaway: Training changes the model; inference uses it; context and retrieval supply information.

Source: Attached chapter, §§1.2 and 1.7.

SLIDE S53SKILLSSECTION — Skills
Page 109 / 198
A reusable kit of reading, guided practice and self-assessment cards, organised with a resource folder above an open textbook.

SLIDE S53SKILLS — Agent Skills: reusable workflows
Page 110 / 198
A skill packages a method, resources and optional code for a compatible agent.
Informal shortcut
A label such as /eli5 asks for a style of response. Its meaning comes from the prompt.
Formal action
A product feature exposed in its interface, such as Study. Availability depends on the surface.
Agent Skill
A folder with SKILL.md, instructions and optional references, templates or scripts.
The host discovers a skill, loads its instructions and uses the tools it actually has.
[SKILL_STANDARD] Agent Skills. Specification: SKILL.md format, metadata, compatibility and optional resources.
https://agentskills.io/specification
An open file format does not guarantee that every script or tool works in every host.
[SKILL_OPENAI] OpenAI. Skills: reusable workflows in ChatGPT and Codex plugins.
https://developers.openai.com/plugins/concepts/skills
A skill supplies procedures; an MCP-backed app supplies external tools and data.

SLIDE S53SKILLHOSTS — Where skills can run
Page 111 / 198
Check the product and the runtime before choosing a skill.
ChatGPT
Recorded here in ChatGPT web: Plugins → Skills → upload a package, then select it in Work mode. Availability depends on the account and workspace.
Coding agents
Codex and other compatible agents can load skill packages. Scripts may require Python, Node or network access.
A shared file format does not guarantee identical tools, installation or results.
[SKILL_CHATGPT] OpenAI. Skills in ChatGPT. Help Center, accessed 5 November 2026.
https://help.openai.com/en/articles/20001066-skills-in-chatgpt
Availability is limited to eligible accounts and workspace settings; installation and syncing differ by surface.
[SKILL_STANDARD] Agent Skills. Specification: SKILL.md format, metadata, compatibility and optional resources.
https://agentskills.io/specification
An open file format does not guarantee that every script or tool works in every host.

learn I am Andrea, a second-year computer science student studying the attached workshop book, Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence. Help me understand training, ordinary inference and RAG using only §§1.2 and 1.7. I keep thinking that adding a definition to a prompt updates the weights. Start with one diagnostic question and wait for my answer. Keep each turn under 100 words and cite the chapter section. Answer in English.

Ha lavorato per 13s
ChatGPT ha detto:

Andrea, suppose you add “In this chapter, X means Y” to a prompt, and the model then uses that definition correctly. What do you think changed inside the system, and why does that explain its answer? (§1.2)

Hai detto:
I think its weights changed because it learned the new definition from my message.
ChatGPT ha detto:

You’ve identified that the message affects the answer, but using new information does not mean updating weights. Your definition becomes part of the conversation context. Ordinary inference uses that context with fixed parameters; training uses prediction error and optimisation to change parameters. (§1.2)

Suppose a RAG system retrieves a page containing the same definition and supplies it as context. Would that require changing the model’s weights? (§1.7)

Hai detto:
No. RAG supplies retrieved passages as context to ordinary inference. The weights stay fixed. Training or fine-tuning changes parameters through optimisation. Adding a definition changes the available context, not the parameters.
ChatGPT ha detto:

That’s correct. You’ve separated changes to context from changes to parameters: training changes parameters; ordinary inference uses fixed parameters; RAG supplies retrieved passages as context. (§§1.2, 1.7)

One final transfer check: a RAG system retrieves a recent document and gives an incorrect answer. Does the document’s recency establish that the answer is correct? Explain one way the process could fail. (§1.7)

RECORDED CONVERSATION / GENERATED FILE
Generated text file:

How do training and ordinary inference differ in their use of model parameters? | Training updates parameters through optimisation to reduce prediction error. Ordinary inference calculates outputs using fixed, already available parameters. (§1.2)
When Andrea adds a new definition to a prompt, what changes, and what does using that definition demonstrate about model weights? | The definition changes the context available to current inference. Using it does not demonstrate that model weights have changed. (§1.2)
How does retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) supply new information without a parameter update? | RAG retrieves selected document passages and supplies them as generation context. The consulted collection and model parameters are distinct components, so weights can remain unchanged. (§1.7)

scientific critical thinking Using only the attached workshop book, review Andrea’s explanation: "Adding the chapter to a prompt updates model weights. RAG retrieves passages and therefore guarantees a correct answer. Increasing temperature adds knowledge." Make a compact table: claim, verdict, chapter evidence, correction. Use §§1.2, 1.6 and 1.7. Distinguish what the chapter states from anything not established. End with one question Andrea should answer independently. Keep the response under 220 words. Answer in English.

Ha lavorato per 19s
ChatGPT ha detto:
Claim	Verdict	Chapter evidence	Correction
“Adding the chapter to a prompt updates model weights.”	Incorrect for ordinary inference.	§1.2 distinguishes optimisation that updates parameters from new messages that change available context. §1.7 says documents can supply information without changing weights.	Adding the chapter changes the context. Using its information does not establish that weights changed. Any later service use of data is a separate matter (§1.2).
“RAG retrieves passages and therefore guarantees a correct answer.”	Retrieval is described correctly; the guarantee is contradicted.	§1.7 states that retrieval can fail, select irrelevant passages, or supply material that the response misinterprets.	RAG supplies selected passages to generation. Correctness remains unestablished until the generated claim is checked against supporting evidence; a link alone is insufficient (§1.7).
“Increasing temperature adds knowledge.”	Explicitly contradicted.	§1.6 states that, with logits fixed, higher temperature makes the token distribution more uniform; it adds no knowledge and does not retrain the model.	Temperature changes probabilities used for token selection. It does not establish greater knowledge, truthfulness, or originality in an individual answer (§1.6).

Independent question: Andrea, if an answer changes after you add a chapter passage and raise temperature, how would you isolate each intervention’s effect, and what could you conclude about weights and correctness?

SLIDE S58PROFESSOR — Professor: request an explanation
Page 126 / 198
Ask for a short explanation and example of training versus inference.
Check parameter updates and the limits of the analogy against the assigned chapter.
Real Goblin Tools Professor execution for Andrea’s GenAI study task.
Real execution · 17 s
Professor — real execution, recorded 5 November 2026

INPUT
Training versus inference in a language model. Explain the difference briefly and give one short example.

ACTUAL RESULT
EXPLANATION
Training a language model means teaching it by showing lots of text so it learns patterns and how words fit together. Inference is when the model uses what it learned to generate or predict text based on new input.

Example: Training is like studying grammar and vocabulary. Inference is like writing a sentence using what you’ve learned.

EXAMPLE FIELD
Training: Teaching the model to understand language by showing it millions of sentences and adjusting its internal settings.
Example: Feeding a model thousands of news articles to help it learn grammar and facts.

Inference: Using the trained model to generate or understand text without changing its settings.
Example: Asking the model to write a summary of today's news after it has already been trained.

00:00:00.000 Ask a focused question about training and inference.
00:00:04.000 Professor generates an explanation and an example.
00:00:07.000 Read the actual explanation; an analogy still needs checking.
00:00:11.000 Return to the chapter: which parameters change, and when?
[TOOL_GOBLIN_PROFESSOR] Goblin Tools. (2026). Professor. Official help and real execution recorded 5 November 2026.
https://goblin.tools/Professor
Generated explanations and analogies need checking against the assigned chapter.

SLIDE S62 — Ask a focused question
Page 135 / 198
Request a short explanation with references to the chapter
The need is to understand why an answer can change without retraining.
Prompt / excerpt: Use only the chapter. In three short bullets, explain the difference between learned parameters and context during ordinary inference. Cite the relevant passages.
Read the answer, then check whether each reference supports its claim.
NotebookLM · source-based chat: real actions recorded from scratch in @Browser.
Recorded in @Browser · 10 s
NotebookLM · source-based chat — recorded from scratch in @Browser, 5 November 2026

Authentic UI actions and playback, with generation waits cut between scenes.
The interface recording is silent; English captions are available.

OBSERVED RESULT
* **Definition and role**: **Learned parameters** are internal numerical values (weights) established during training to guide model transformations, whereas **context** is the sequence of text and instructions made available specifically for the current inference step.
* **Variability during inference**: During ordinary inference, the **model's parameters remain fixed**, while the **context changes dynamically** with each new input or message.
* **Effect on model state**: Adding or modifying information in the context alters the input data for that specific calculation but **does not update or retrain the underlying weights**.

💡 Would you like to explore how temperature settings alter output probabilities while keeping both the model parameters and context fixed?

SCOPE
Question submitted and complete answer recorded from the uploaded chapter PDF in @Browser.

OBSERVED RESULT
Question: A developer supplies 'In this interaction, X means Y' in a prompt. Another user starts a fresh conversation with the same model. What happens to the custom definition?

Selected C: It will revert to its original default understanding because passing information in a prompt context does not update the model's learned parameters.

Feedback: That's right! Context changes only supply transient information available during a specific inference step; altering prompt inputs does not modify or re-train the underlying parameter weights of the model.

Saved note: Model Parameters and Context.

* **Definition and role**: **Learned parameters** are internal numerical values (weights) established during training to guide model transformations, whereas **context** is the sequence of text and instructions made available specifically for the current inference step.
* **Variability during inference**: During ordinary inference, the **model's parameters remain fixed**, while the **context changes dynamically** with each new input or message.
* **Effect on model state**: Adding or modifying information in the context alters the input data for that specific calculation but **does not update or retrain the underlying weights**.

OBSERVED RESULT
Study Guide: Foundations of Generative Artificial Intelligence

This study guide provides a detailed review of core concepts in generative artificial intelligence, autoregressive language models, and Transformer decoder architecture based on the foundational curriculum by Marco Cremaschi.

1. Core Concepts Summary

Generative AI vs. AI Assistants

* Generative AI Systems: Broadly defined as systems that produce content (such as text or images) conditioned on an input. "Generative" describes the activity itself rather than a single model architecture.
* Autoregressive Language Models: Models that calculate the probability distribution of which token follows an existing sequence of text (context). Continuously repeating this next-token prediction step generates extended text.
* Model vs. Assistant: A language model calculates next-token probabilities using fixed parameters. An assistant application wraps around the model and includes additional service-managed components such as system instructions, web search capabilities, document retrieval (RAG), content filters, external tools, and conversation memory.

Data, Parameters, and System Training

* Parameters: Numerical values used in the model’s internal mathematical transformations. Parameters do not exist as a human-readable encyclopedia prior to training.
* Pretraining: The optimization process where a model compares its predicted next-token distributions against actual text examples, updating parameter weights to minimize aggregate prediction error.
* Instruction Tuning / Alignment: Post-pretraining stages (e.g., using demonstrations and human preferences as in InstructGPT) that adapt a model to follow instructions and exhibit desired behaviors.
* Inference: Running calculations using fixed, already-available parameters. Inserting text into a conversation prompt alters the context of the current inference; it does not update the underlying model parameters or weights.

+--------------------------+-------------------------------------------------------------+
| Intervention             | Primary Effect                                              |
+--------------------------+-------------------------------------------------------------+
| Training / Fine-tuning   | Modifies the model's internal parameter weights.            |
| Context Message          | Alters information available during the current inference.  |
| Document Retrieval (RAG) | Supplies external text passages into the active context.    |
+--------------------------+-------------------------------------------------------------+


Tokens, Embeddings, and Architectural Flow

* Tokenizer: Converts input strings into sequences of numerical identifiers (token IDs). Tokens can represent words, sub-words, punctuation, or spaces. Token counts measure context length.
* Token IDs: Indices within a vocabulary dictionary. Numerical proximity between IDs does not signify semantic similarity.
* Initial Embedding: A static, learned vector retrieved from a lookup table using a token's numerical ID.
* Contextual Representation: A dynamic vector produced after initial embeddings pass through Transformer decoder blocks, incorporating relationships with other permitted tokens in the sequence.
* Positional Encoding: Methods (such as Rotary Position Embeddings / RoPE in Qwen2.5) that inject token order information into representations.
* Transformer Decoder: A multi-layered structure comprising causal attention mechanisms, feed-forward networks, residual connections, and layer normalizations.
* Causal Attention: Computes Query (q), Key (k), and Value (v) vectors across permitted positions. A causal mask prevents current sequence positions from accessing future tokens during next-token prediction.

Probabilities, Softmax, and Temperature

* Logit (z): Unnormalized numerical output scores produced by the final decoder layer for every entry in the vocabulary.
* Softmax Function: Normalizes raw logits into a valid non-negative probability distribution summing to 1 across the vocabulary: \ p_i = \frac{\exp(z_i / T)}{\sum_j \exp(z_j / T)} \
* Temperature (T): A positive scaling factor applied to logits before softmax normalization.
  * Lower Temperature (T < 1.0): Concentrates probability mass onto the highest-scoring tokens.
  * Higher Temperature (T > 1.0): Flattens the distribution, making token probabilities more uniform.
  * Properties: Temperature scaling alters distribution concentration without changing the relative ranking order of the logits, adding new knowledge, or updating model weights.
* Decoding Strategies:
  * Greedy Selection: Continuously selects the single highest-probability token.
  * Sampling: Randomly selects tokens based on the output probability distribution, allowing varied outputs across runs. Can be constrained via top-k or top-p filters.

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and Limitations

* Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): A framework where an external search component retrieves passages from a document collection and feeds them into the model's input context. RAG provides updated or specific context without fine-tuning model weights.
* Hallucinations: Plausible-sounding but incorrect, ungrounded, or unsupported generations. Lowering temperature does not eliminate hallucinations; deterministic decoding can consistently repeat the same false statement.

2. Short-Answer Quiz

1. What is the operational distinction between a standalone language model and a complete AI assistant application?
2. How does the function of pretraining differ from inserting new information directly into a user prompt?
3. Why is a token’s numerical ID not considered a measurement of its semantic meaning?
4. What is the architectural difference between an initial token embedding and a contextual representation?
5. What constraint defines "causal" attention within a Transformer decoder block?
6. How does altering the temperature parameter (T) affect the softmax output distribution over a fixed set of logits?
7. How does greedy selection differ from probabilistic sampling during generation?
8. Does retrieving external documents via Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) update a model's parameters? Explain.
9. Why does lowering the temperature parameter fail to eliminate model hallucinations?
10. In experimental evaluations of language models, why is it necessary to isolate variables such as temperature and input context?

3. Quiz Answer Key

1. What is the operational distinction between a standalone language model and a complete AI assistant application? Answer: A language model is an autoregressive system that assigns probabilities to token sequences and predicts next tokens using internal parameter calculations. An assistant application is a broader system built around the model, incorporating external features such as system prompts, search engines, document retrieval components, content filters, memory management, and external tools.
2. How does the function of pretraining differ from inserting new information directly into a user prompt? Answer: Pretraining updates the model's internal parameter weights by optimizing predictions against broad text datasets to reduce aggregate error. Inserting information into a prompt merely provides data within the context window for the current inference step, leaving the underlying parameter weights completely unchanged.
3. Why is a token’s numerical ID not considered a measurement of its semantic meaning? Answer: Token IDs serve strictly as arbitrary lookup indices within a tokenizer's fixed vocabulary dictionary. Consecutive or nearby numerical IDs do not reflect conceptual similarity, and individual vector coordinates or ID numbers do not map to isolated human semantic properties.
4. What is the architectural difference between an initial token embedding and a contextual representation? Answer: An initial embedding is a static, learned vector retrieved directly from the vocabulary matrix using a token's numerical ID. A contextual representation is the dynamic vector produced after passing the initial embedding through Transformer decoder blocks, integrating positional encodings and causal attention from other permitted sequence tokens.
5. What constraint defines "causal" attention within a Transformer decoder block? Answer: Causal attention enforces a mathematical masking constraint that prevents a given token position from attending to subsequent or "future" token positions in the sequence. When predicting the next token, a position can only extract and process information from earlier tokens and its own available state.
6. How does altering the temperature parameter (T) affect the softmax output distribution over a fixed set of logits? Answer: Raising the temperature distributes probability mass more uniformly across the vocabulary, whereas lowering the temperature concentrates probability mass onto the highest-scoring tokens. Crucially, adjusting temperature scales the logits before normalization without changing their relative rank order or adding external knowledge to the system.
7. How does greedy selection differ from probabilistic sampling during generation? Answer: Greedy selection deterministically chooses the single token with the absolute highest probability at each sequence step. Sampling selects next tokens stochastically based on the output probability distribution, allowing different generation runs to produce diverging responses from identical inputs.
8. Does retrieving external documents via Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) update a model's parameters? Explain. Answer: No, RAG does not update model parameters. RAG extracts relevant textual passages from an external database and appends them directly into the prompt context, allowing the fixed model to process the retrieved text during standard inference.
9. Why does lowering the temperature parameter fail to eliminate model hallucinations? Answer: Hallucinations stem from underlying structural, data, and statistical generation limits rather than temperature settings alone. Lowering temperature simply forces the model to select its highest-probability tokens more deterministically, which can lead it to repeat the exact same factual error with higher consistency.
10. In experimental evaluations of language models, why is it necessary to isolate variables such as temperature and input context? Answer: Isolating single variables ensures that observed changes in generation outputs or probability distributions can be definitively attributed to a specific cause. Changing temperature and input context simultaneously confounds experimental results, making it impossible to separate parameter-scaling effects from context-driven shift effects.

4. Essay Practice Questions

(Note: These questions are designed for extended analysis and independent study. Answers are not provided.)

1. Parameters vs. Contextual Knowledge: Contrast how a language model utilizes knowledge stored in its static parameters with information provided dynamically within its context window. In your analysis, discuss the implications of this distinction for system design, fine-tuning (e.g., InstructGPT), and real-time data updating.
2. End-to-End Technical Pipeline of a Generation Step: Trace the complete mathematical and architectural path of an input string through a Transformer decoder model during a single autoregressive step. Begin at string tokenization and cover initial embeddings, positional encodings, Query/Key/Value causal attention matrices, feed-forward transformations, raw logits, temperature scaling, softmax normalization, and final token selection.
3. Mathematical Behavior of Softmax and Temperature: Detail the mathematical operation of the softmax function p_i = \frac{\exp(z_i / T)}{\sum_j \exp(z_j / T)}. Using a hypothetical or empirical set of logits, analyze how varying T between low (T < 1.0) and high (T > 1.0) values alters total distribution entropy, top-token probability concentration, and candidate token selection probability without shifting logit rankings.
4. Architectural Limitations, Verifiability, and RAG: Analyze the root causes of model hallucinations and factual inaccuracies in autoregressive models. Evaluate how Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses parametric knowledge freshness and factual grounding, while highlighting specific modes of failure that RAG systems remain vulnerable to (such as retrieval errors or context misinterpretation).
5. Experimental Methodology for LLM Behavior: Formulate a rigorous empirical experimental protocol for evaluating a local quantized language model (such as Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct running via Transformers.js). Define your control parameters, primary dependent variables (including full-vocabulary probability mass and next-token distributions), hypothesis verification techniques, and code auditing procedures to detect silent logic errors in model execution pipelines.

5. Comprehensive Glossary

* Causal Attention: An attention mechanism variant restricted by a causal mask, ensuring that predictions for a given sequence position depend strictly on preceding and current positions, blocking access to future tokens.
* Context: The full sequence of tokens (including system instructions, user inputs, historical dialogue, and retrieved documents) made available to a language model during a single inference step.
* Contextual Representation: A dynamic, high-dimensional vector derived by passing initial embeddings through decoder layers, encoding both token identity and relationships to surrounding sequence context.
* Embedding: A static, high-dimensional learned numerical vector associated with a specific token ID in a model's vocabulary lookup table.
* Fine-Tuning: A post-pretraining process that further trains a model on specialized datasets or preference evaluations (e.g., RLHF / InstructGPT), updating parameter weights to align model outputs with desired behaviors or domains.
* Generative AI: Systems designed to generate synthetic content (text, images, audio, etc.) conditioned on specific input prompts or instructions.
* Greedy Selection: A deterministic decoding procedure that always selects the token assigned the highest probability by the softmax distribution.
* Inference: The process of executing a trained model to generate predictions or outputs using fixed, existing parameter weights.
* Logit (z): Unnormalized numerical output scores produced by the final linear layer of a neural network before conversion into probabilities.
* Large Language Model (LLM): An autoregressive deep neural network trained on vast text datasets to compute token sequence probabilities; it refers to the core model architecture rather than an entire software application wrapper.
* Parameter: A learned numerical weight or bias within a model's neural network layers that dictates its structural transformations and knowledge encoding, updated during training.
* Quantisation: A compression technique that lowers the numerical precision of model parameters (e.g., from 16-bit floating-point to 4-bit integers / q4), reducing memory footprint and computational requirements.
* Query, Key, Value (Q, K, V): Linear projections of token representations within an attention layer. Queries are matched against Keys to calculate normalized attention weights, which are subsequently used to compute a weighted sum over Values.
* Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): An application architecture that combines search algorithms with text generation by retrieving relevant passages from an external collection and inserting them into the model's inference context window.
* Sampling: The process of stochastically selecting a output token from the calculated softmax probability distribution.
* Softmax: A mathematical function that converts a vector of arbitrary real-valued logits into a normalized probability distribution of non-negative values that sum to 1.
* Temperature (T): A positive scaling hyperparameter applied to raw logits prior to softmax normalization that controls the randomness or concentration of the output probability distribution.
* Token: The fundamental unit of text processing in a language model, representing a word, sub-word segment, character, or special structural symbol.
* Tokenizer: An algorithm and vocabulary mapping tool responsible for segmenting raw text strings into numerical token IDs and converting token IDs back into human-readable text.

SLIDE S81TEACHERTOOLS — Tools for teachers

SLIDE S82 — The teacher designs the evidence
Page 167 / 198
The goal, task and criterion must concern the same knowledge
Goal
Understand the roles of context, parameters and token selection.
Evidence
An explanation, commented diagram, justified prediction and correction.
Criteria
Concepts and relationships; process explanation; application and checking.
Assess what the learner can justify, alongside the assisted product.

Assess GenAI knowledge, without rewarding only polished prose or code. Distinguish the assisted product from the independent explanation.

SLIDE S84 — Review the rubric descriptors
Page 170 / 198
A constructed revision example, not automated assessment
Keep three criteria separate and apply the same rubric to the responses.
Too general
“Shows a good understanding of the topic”.
Observable
“Distinguishes parameters from context and explains why changing a prompt is not retraining”.
The rubric stays in the kit without Formative’s paid integrated rubric feature.
[TOOL_FORMATIVE] Formative. (2026, 4 maggio). What Formative plan is best for me?
https://help.formative.com/en/articles/6827953-what-formative-plan-is-best-for-me
Free includes multiple choice, open responses, drawing and teacher feedback. Integrated rubrics, audio/video responses and Luna feedback are paid features.

SLIDE S84A — Rubric: concepts and relationships
Page 171 / 198
Criterion 1 · four levels of evidence
1. 1 · Needs reconstruction
Confuses training and inference, or treats probabilities as truth.
2. 2 · Partial
Names concepts, but incompletely relates parameters and context.
3. 3 · Coherent
Distinguishes phases and states correct relationships.
4. 4 · Justified
Justifies relationships and identifies conditions and limits in a new example.
Apply the criterion to meaning in the agreed response mode.

SLIDE S84B — Rubric: process explanation
Page 172 / 198
Criterion 2 · make the links between stages explicit
1. 1 · Absent
Repeats output without describing the stages.
2. 2 · Fragmentary
Describes some stages but leaves gaps or reversals.
3. 3 · Ordered
Connects input, tokens, processing, distribution and selection.
4. 4 · Justified
Explains the links and separates the diagram from observed data.
A commented diagram or oral explanation can reveal the same process.

SLIDE S84C — Rubric: application and checking
Page 173 / 198
Criterion 3 · predict, test and revise
1. 1 · Unjustified
States a conclusion without a prediction or check.
2. 2 · Incomplete
Predicts, but changes multiple conditions or does not compare outcomes.
3. 3 · Controlled
Varies one condition, compares the outcome and corrects an error.
4. 4 · Critical
Justifies revision and recognises sampling, limitations and uncertainty.
Do not reward a lucky outcome from one generation.

SLIDE S85 — Prepare questions and expected answers
Page 174 / 198
The first question checks a fundamental distinction
Q1 · During ordinary inference, what changes when a new prompt is entered? A: original training data. B: calculation context. C: learned parameters.
Expected: B, with justification; a correct choice alone provides limited evidence.
Prompt / excerpt: Generate five chapter-based questions: distinction, explanation, diagram, prediction and transfer. For each, provide the expected answer, a detectable misconception and the rubric criterion. Avoid questions answered by copying a definition.
A teacher-reviewed teaching example, not a validated test.

SLIDE S85B — Explanation and diagram completion
Page 175 / 198
Questions 2 and 3 · observe the connections
Q2 · Explain
Why does a token not always match a word? Expected: encoding may split words or represent other sequence elements.
Q3 · Complete a diagram
Complete text → tokens → model → distribution → selection. What repeats? Expected: the selected token joins the context and calculation continues.
Read correctness and explanation without requiring code.

SLIDE S85C — Prediction and transfer
Page 176 / 198
Questions 4 and 5 · apply a concept and recognise an error
Q4 · Predict
Same input and scores: what changes from T = 0.5 to T = 1.2? Expected: less concentrated probabilities, without a guarantee about one sampled token.
Q5 · Correct an error
“The most probable alternative will be true”. Refute using a new example. Expected: token plausibility differs from content truth.
Keep the initial prediction, test outcome and justified revision.

SLIDE S88 — Read three simulated responses

Profile A · complete product

Profile B · vocabulary present
Names tokens and probabilities, but omits the link between distribution and selection.

SLIDE S89 — Feedback that specifies the next action
Page 181 / 198
Teacher-written examples address the observed difficulty
For profile A
Revisit parameters versus context. Correct the arrow and explain why.
For profile B
Add the selection rule between distribution and token. Explain the link.
For profile C
Transfer your explanation to a new context and identify what remains uncertain.
Teacher-written feedback in the Free plan, followed by a new learner attempt.

SLIDE S89A — Activity: adapt an assessment
Page 182 / 198
Five minutes on your subject and an access barrier
1. Choose a goal · 1 minute
Name a concept you teach or study.
2. Design evidence · 2 minutes
Write a task and observable criterion, allowing AI to produce the initial product.
3. Remove a barrier · 2 minutes
Offer an equivalent access or response option; share the reason for your choice.
A reusable example with a coherent goal, evidence and support.

SLIDE S90ACCESSIBILITYSECTION — Accessibility

SLIDE S90 — Accessibility and learning differences
Page 184 / 198
Start with the barrier in the actual task, with the learner
Reading and fatigue
Adaptable text, read aloud, segments and pauses.
Writing the response
Dictation, review or another agreed response mode.
Organisation and calculation
A visible plan, glossary, diagrams and goal-aligned supports.
Dyslexia and other specific learning difficulties vary; attention needs are distinct. No typeface or tool fits everyone.

SLIDE S95 — Reuse the learning path in another subject
Page 191 / 198
Keep the sequence; change the content and evidence
1. Start with a source and a goal
For example: understand a historical interpretation from an assigned chapter.
2. Make ideas visible
Record an initial explanation, uncertainties and a manageable plan.
3. Use tools, then check
Compare a generated explanation with the source and revise relationships.
4. Demonstrate and reflect
Explain a new example independently; identify the next learning step.
Choose a tool for a specific need and decide what evidence it should leave.

SLIDE S96 — The evidence collected along the way
Page 192 / 198
A product and an explanation answer different assessment needs
Learning process
Selected plan, checked passages, a generated Mind Map and self-check responses.
Assisted product
Diagram, application, recorded tests and revision history.
Demonstrated knowledge
Independent explanation, prediction, error correction and reflection.
Compare these records before deciding what the learner needs next.

[TOOL_NOTEBOOK_PRICE] Google. (2026). Learn about Gemini Notebook’s plans; Manage your Gemini Notebook usage limits.
https://support.google.com/gemininotebook/answer/16213268?hl=en
Standard access is free with an account and variable usage limits. Education eligibility depends on the actual licence.
[TOOL_NAPKIN] Napkin AI. (2026). Pricing.
https://www.napkin.ai/pricing.html
Free: 500 credits/week and branded PNG/PDF export. SVG/PPT export is paid.
[TOOL_MINDMEISTER] MindMeister. (2026). Pricing.
https://www.mindmeister.com/pricing
Free allows three maps; Personal/Pro add unlimited maps and plan-specific exports.
[TOOL_REMNOTE] RemNote. (2026). Pricing.
https://www.remnote.com/pricing
Free notes and flashcards; advanced PDF, image-occlusion and AI features depend on plan.
[TOOL_READWRITE] Everway. (2026). Read&Write education pricing.
https://www.everway.com/products/read-and-write-education/pricing/
Normally licensed; free access for eligible K–12 teachers is conditional. Languages and functions vary by platform.
[TOOL_WOLFRAM_PRICE] Wolfram Alpha LLC. (2026). Wolfram|Alpha Pro pricing plans.
https://www.wolframalpha.com/pro/pricing/
Basic is free with sign-in and does not include step-by-step solutions. Pro includes step-by-step solutions for supported problems; plan, region and problem coverage matter.
[TOOL_MAGIC_PRICE] MagicSchool. (2026). Pricing and plans.
https://www.magicschool.ai/pricing
Free has limits; Plus and institutional licences are distinct.
[TOOL_FORMATIVE] Formative. (2026, 4 maggio). What Formative plan is best for me?
https://help.formative.com/en/articles/6827953-what-formative-plan-is-best-for-me
Free includes multiple choice, open responses, drawing and teacher feedback. Integrated rubrics, audio/video responses and Luna feedback are paid features.
[TOOL_WAYGROUND] Wayground. (2026). Wayground Starter (Basic) plan.
https://help.wayground.com/support/solutions/articles/158000404038-wayground-starter-basic-plan
Limits and features vary by region; distinguish Starter from premium trials.
[TOOL_MINDMEISTER_LIMIT] MindMeister · Basic map limit. Reviewed 5 November 2026.
https://support.mindmeister.com/hc/en-us/articles/39422924662546-Changes-to-team-maps-and-map-limits
Account required. No authenticated chapter map was created; paid plans remove the map-count limit.
